Q: Mom buys 70 white t - shirts in total. If white t - shirts can be purchased in packages and mom buys 14 packages How many white t - shirts does each package have?
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many white t-shirts are in each package. Mom buys a total of 70 t-shirts, and these are divided evenly into 14 packages. The strategy involves dividing the total number of t-shirts by the number of packages.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total number of t-shirts = 70
- Number of packages = 14
- Each package contains the same number of t-shirts (even distribution).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Use direct division. Divide the total number of t-shirts by the number of packages: \( \text{Total t-shirts} \div \text{Number of packages} \).

**Method 2:** Use repeated subtraction. Subtract the number of t-shirts in one package repeatedly until the total reaches zero, then count the number of subtractions.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Direct division**
- Perform \( 70 \div 14 \).
- \( 70 \div 14 = 5 \).
- Each package contains 5 t-shirts.

#### **Method 2: Repeated subtraction**
- Start with 70 t-shirts and subtract 14 repeatedly:
  - \( 70 - 14 = 56 \)
  - \( 56 - 14 = 42 \)
  - \( 42 - 14 = 28 \)
  - \( 28 - 14 = 14 \)
  - \( 14 - 14 = 0 \)
- Count the number of subtractions: 5 subtractions.
- Each package contains 5 t-shirts.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is faster and more efficient, as it directly computes the result using division.
- **Method 2** is valid but unnecessarily time-consuming for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
Using direct division:
- \( 70 \div 14 = 5 \).
- Each package contains 5 t-shirts.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Multiply the number of t-shirts per package by the number of packages: \( 5 \times 14 = 70 \).
- The total matches the original number of t-shirts, confirming the result.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **5**.
EXTRACTED:
5.0
GROUND_TRUTH:

Q: Winter is almost here and most animals are migrating to warmer countries. There were 89 bird families living near the mountain. If 60 bird families flew away for winter How many more bird families flew away for the winter than those that stayed behind?
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding how many more bird families flew away for the winter than those that stayed behind. To do this, we first calculate the number of bird families that stayed behind by subtracting the number that flew away from the total. Then, we find the difference between the number of bird families that flew away and those that stayed.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total bird families: 89
- Bird families that flew away: 60
- Bird families that stayed behind = Total - Flew away = \( 89 - 60 \)
- We assume no other bird families left or arrived.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
1. **Method 1 (Direct Calculation):**
   - First, calculate the number of bird families that stayed behind: \( 89 - 60 \).
   - Then, find the difference between the number of bird families that flew away and those that stayed: \( 60 - \text{(stayed behind)} \).

2. **Method 2 (Complementary Approach):**
   - Directly calculate the difference between the number of bird families that flew away and the total number of bird families, then subtract twice the number of bird families that stayed behind: \( 60 - (89 - 60) \).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### Method 1:
1. Calculate the number of bird families that stayed behind:
   \[
   89 - 60 = 29
   \]
2. Calculate the difference between the number of bird families that flew away and those that stayed:
   \[
   60 - 29 = 31
   \]

#### Method 2:
1. Directly calculate the difference:
   \[
   60 - (89 - 60) = 60 - 29 = 31
   \]

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods:
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more intuitive and easier to follow because it breaks the problem into smaller, logical steps. **Method 2** is valid but introduces unnecessary complexity.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1):
1. Calculate the number of bird families that stayed behind:
   \[
   89 - 60 = 29
   \]
2. Calculate the difference between the number of bird families that flew away and those that stayed:
   \[
   60 - 29 = 31
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check:
- Add the number of bird families that stayed and the difference:
  \[
  29 + 31 = 60 \quad \text{(matches the number of bird families that flew away)} ✅
  \]
- Add the number of bird families that stayed and flew away:
  \[
  29 + 60 = 89 \quad \text{(matches the total number of bird families)} ✅
  \]

---

### Final Answer:
The number of bird families that flew away for the winter is **31 more** than those that stayed behind.  
The answer is **31**.

Q: Paco had 36 cookies. He gave 14 cookies to his friend and ate 10 cookies. How many cookies did Paco have left?
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many cookies Paco has left after giving some to his friend and eating some himself. This involves subtracting the number of cookies given away and eaten from the initial total. A straightforward subtraction approach will suffice.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial number of cookies: 36
- Cookies given to a friend: 14
- Cookies eaten by Paco: 10
- No other cookies are added or removed.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
1. **Method 1:** Subtract the cookies given away first, then subtract the cookies eaten.
2. **Method 2:** Combine the cookies given away and eaten into a single total, then subtract this total from the initial number of cookies.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### Method 1:
1. Subtract the cookies given to the friend:  
   \( 36 - 14 = 22 \)
2. Subtract the cookies eaten:  
   \( 22 - 10 = 12 \)

#### Method 2:
1. Combine the cookies given away and eaten:  
   \( 14 + 10 = 24 \)
2. Subtract the combined total from the initial number of cookies:  
   \( 36 - 24 = 12 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods:
- Both methods yield the same result of 12 cookies.
- **Method 2** is more efficient because it reduces the number of subtraction steps by combining the losses first.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 2):
1. Combine the cookies given away and eaten:  
   \( 14 + 10 = 24 \)
2. Subtract the combined total from the initial number of cookies:  
   \( 36 - 24 = 12 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result:
- Add the cookies given away and eaten to the remaining cookies:  
  \( 12 + 14 + 10 = 36 \), which matches the initial total. ✅

---

The answer is **12**.
EXTRACTED:
12.0

\[
34 + 18 = 52
\]

- Break \( 34 \) into \( 30 + 4 \).
- Break \( 18 \) into \( 10 + 8 \).

- Add the ones: \( 4 + 8 = 12 \).
- Combine the results: \( 40 + 12 = 52 \).

### Step 4: Compare both methods:
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it involves fewer steps.
- **Method 2** is valid but introduces unnecessary complexity for this problem.

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1):

\]

Q: Josh had some marbles in his collection. He lost 21 marbles. If he has 12 marbles now How many marbles did he have in his collection?
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the original number of marbles Josh had in his collection before he lost 21 marbles. This requires adding the number of marbles he currently has (12) to the number of marbles he lost (21). The strategy involves reversing the subtraction operation that resulted in the current count.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Current number of marbles: 12
- Marbles lost: 21
- No other marbles were added or removed from the collection.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Use direct addition. Add the number of marbles Josh currently has to the number of marbles he lost to find the original total.  
**Method 2:** Use an equation. Represent the original number of marbles as \( x \), and solve the equation \( x - 21 = 12 \) by isolating \( x \).

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Direct addition**
- Add the current number of marbles (12) to the number of marbles lost (21):  
  \[
  12 + 21 = 33
  \]

#### **Method 2: Solve the equation**
- Start with the equation \( x - 21 = 12 \).  
- Add 21 to both sides to isolate \( x \):  
  \[
  x = 12 + 21
  \]
- Compute the result:  
  \[
  x = 33
  \]

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more straightforward and avoids the need for setting up an equation. It is simpler and faster for this type of problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
Using **Method 1**, add the current number of marbles (12) to the number lost (21):  
\[
12 + 21 = 33
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
Verify by subtracting the lost marbles (21) from the original total (33):  
\[
33 - 21 = 12
\]  
This matches the given current count, confirming the solution is correct.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **33**.
EXTRACTED:
33.0
